; Inverse: [1 0 -1   0 1 0   0 0 1]
; NOTE: The two input cells have different centering types (I and C) but the transformation
;       matrix has determinant 1. This occurs because a centered
;       lattice is always constructed by adding extra lattice points
;       to a primitive lattice. The primitive sublattice of the
;       centered cell matches the primitive cell exactly, which is
;       why P3 distance is zero. This is not the same as two
;       lattices being equivalent -- the centered cell has more

;       A non-zero value indicates the lattices have different
;       point densities despite the perfect P3 match.

;       matrix has determinant 1. This occurs because a centered
;       lattice is always constructed by adding extra lattice points

;       centered cell matches the primitive cell exactly, which is
;       why P3 distance is zero. This is not the same as two
;       lattices being equivalent -- the centered cell has more

=== TEST: PvsIDegenerate ===
Description: P vs I identical conventional parameters: a centered lattice is always generated
by adding points to a primitive lattice, so the P cell is always a sublattice of
the I cell with the same parameters. The perfect match (P3~0) is correct and
expected -- this relationship is not a supercell in the usual sense but is the
fundamental centering relationship. NCDist distance=167 distinguishes this from a true
equivalence between identical lattices.
Input cells:
  Cell 1 (P):    10.000    12.000    14.000    90.000    90.000    90.000 
  Cell 2 (I):    10.000    12.000    14.000    90.000    90.000    90.000

; Inverse: [-1 0 0   0 1 0   0 0 -1]

;       matrix has determinant 1. This occurs because a centered
;       lattice is always constructed by adding extra lattice points
